Well… I guess my family thought it was getting a little out of hand. I was working on the house, putting up wood and lights and display things, trying to make it really something special. They say I used up our savings on it, and I kept going after dark.

I didn’t mean any harm, doctor. It felt important at the time, like if I stopped, the house—maybe even the whole block—would miss its chance. That probably sounds foolish, I know… but that’s why they wanted me to come in.

But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be done in a very specific way to be considered complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it had to be a very specific way, doctor. Not like I had a written plan or rules in my head, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt was more… I kept feeling like it still wasn’t quite finished, so I’d add another thing or fix something that looked off. But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. I was working on the house, putting up wood and lights and display things, trying to make it really something special. That probably sounds foolish, I know… but that’s why they wanted me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to keep working on the house, like you couldn't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I didn’t think of it like I couldn’t stop, exactly. But at the time, I just kept feeling like if I quit for the night, the house—maybe even the whole block—would lose its chance at something important. So I kept pushing, even after dark.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind that you couldn't get rid of while working on the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it thoughts repeating, doctor. Not like a phrase going round and round that I couldn’t get rid of.\n\nIt was more… I just kept thinking about what still needed doing on the house, and it felt hard to set it aside. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were doing something over and over, like putting up the lights or the wood, because you felt you had to, not just because you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe that’s fair to say. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be perfect for this special occasion?\n\nPatient: Well… perfect might be too strong a word, doctor. I didn’t think of it like I couldn’t stop, exactly. But at the time, I just kept feeling like if I quit for the night, the house—maybe even the whole block—would lose its chance at something important. So I kept pushing, even after dark.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind that you couldn't get rid of while working on the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it thoughts repeating, doctor. Not like a phrase going round and round that I couldn’t get rid of.\n\nIt was more… I just kept thinking about what still needed doing on the house, and it felt hard to set it aside. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were doing something over and over, like putting up the lights or the wood, because you felt you had to, not just because you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe that’s fair to say. At the time, though, it didn’t feel like just something I wanted. It felt like if I stopped, the house—maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. Does that make sense, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that kept coming into your mind while you were working on the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not disturbing thoughts, exactly. Nothing like something awful popping into my head that I couldn’t shake.\n\nI was mostly just focused on the house and what needed doing next. Maybe too focused, I can see that. But I wouldn’t want to say I had intrusive thoughts if that’s not the right word. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be perfect for this special occasion?\n\nPatient: Well… perfect might be too strong a word, doctor. I didn’t want it to sound like I was being fussy over every little thing.\n\nBut yes, it felt very important that it be right. I know people think I got mixed up about it, but I was sure our neighborhood had been picked for something special—like my last big civic art piece, if that makes sense. And little things seemed to show it, like trucks slowing down by the house or neighbors looking over. It felt like they knew, too, so I couldn’t just leave it half-done. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be done in a very specific way to be considered complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it had to be a very specific way, doctor. Not like I had a written plan or rules in my head, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt was more… I kept feeling like it still wasn’t quite finished, so I’d add another thing or fix something that looked off. But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. But “everything around me” sounds like more than I’d want to claim. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t really see myself that way. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you generally found yourself to be a perfectionist in other areas of your life, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call myself a perfectionist, doctor. I might be missing something my family would notice, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve generally been that way if I’m not sure. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess my family thought it was getting a little out of hand. I was working on the house, putting up wood and lights and display things, trying to make it really something special. They say I used up our savings on it, and I kept going after dark.\n\nI didn’t mean any harm, doctor. It felt important at the time, like if I stopped, the house—maybe even the whole block—would miss its chance. That probably sounds foolish, I know… but that’s why they wanted me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to keep working on the house, like you couldn't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I didn’t think of it like I couldn’t stop, exactly. It felt more like I shouldn’t stop, if that makes sense.\n\nThey say I spent our savings on the wood and the lights and the display things, and I know that doesn’t look good. But at the time, I just kept feeling like if I quit for the night, the house—maybe even the whole block—would lose its chance at something important. So I kept pushing, even after dark.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind that you couldn't get rid of while working on the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it thoughts repeating, doctor. Not like a phrase going round and round that I couldn’t get rid of.\n\nIt was more… I just kept thinking about what still needed doing on the house, and it felt hard to set it aside. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were doing something over and over, like putting up the lights or the wood, because you felt you had to, not just because you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but I did keep putting up the wood and the lights and all the display things, even after dark.\n\nThey say I used our savings on it, which… I know doesn’t sound responsible. At the time, though, it didn’t feel like just something I wanted. It felt like if I stopped, the house—maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. Does that make sense, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that kept coming into your mind while you were working on the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not disturbing thoughts, exactly. Nothing like something awful popping into my head that I couldn’t shake.\n\nI was mostly just focused on the house and what needed doing next. Maybe too focused, I can see that. But I wouldn’t want to say I had intrusive thoughts if that’s not the right word. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be perfect for this special occasion?\n\nPatient: Well… perfect might be too strong a word, doctor. I didn’t want it to sound like I was being fussy over every little thing.\n\nBut yes, it felt very important that it be right. I know people think I got mixed up about it, but I was sure our neighborhood had been picked for something special—like my last big civic art piece, if that makes sense. And little things seemed to show it, like trucks slowing down by the house or neighbors looking over. It felt like they knew, too, so I couldn’t just leave it half-done. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be done in a very specific way to be considered complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it had to be a very specific way, doctor. Not like I had a written plan or rules in my head, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt was more… I kept feeling like it still wasn’t quite finished, so I’d add another thing or fix something that looked off. But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. But “everything around me” sounds like more than I’d want to claim. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t really see myself that way. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you generally found yourself to be a perfectionist in other areas of your life, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call myself a perfectionist, doctor. I mean… I’ve always liked things to be decent, done properly enough, but not perfect-perfect.\n\nWith the house, maybe I got more particular than usual. But in other areas? I don’t really think so. Is that all right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had recurring thoughts or behaviors like this before, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess my family thought it was getting a little out of hand. I was working on the house, putting up wood and lights and display things, trying to make it really something special. They say I used up our savings on it, and I kept going after dark.\n\nI didn’t mean any harm, doctor. It felt important at the time, like if I stopped, the house—maybe even the whole block—would miss its chance. That probably sounds foolish, I know… but that’s why they wanted me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to keep working on the house, like you couldn't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I didn’t think of it like I couldn’t stop, exactly. It felt more like I shouldn’t stop, if that makes sense.\n\nThey say I spent our savings on the wood and the lights and the display things, and I know that doesn’t look good. But at the time, I just kept feeling like if I quit for the night, the house—maybe even the whole block—would lose its chance at something important. So I kept pushing, even after dark.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind that you couldn't get rid of while working on the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it thoughts repeating, doctor. Not like a phrase going round and round that I couldn’t get rid of.\n\nIt was more… I just kept thinking about what still needed doing on the house, and it felt hard to set it aside. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were doing something over and over, like putting up the lights or the wood, because you felt you had to, not just because you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but I did keep putting up the wood and the lights and all the display things, even after dark.\n\nThey say I used our savings on it, which… I know doesn’t sound responsible. At the time, though, it didn’t feel like just something I wanted. It felt like if I stopped, the house—maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. Does that make sense, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that kept coming into your mind while you were working on the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not disturbing thoughts, exactly. Nothing like something awful popping into my head that I couldn’t shake.\n\nI was mostly just focused on the house and what needed doing next. Maybe too focused, I can see that. But I wouldn’t want to say I had intrusive thoughts if that’s not the right word. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be perfect for this special occasion?\n\nPatient: Well… perfect might be too strong a word, doctor. I didn’t want it to sound like I was being fussy over every little thing.\n\nBut yes, it felt very important that it be right. I know people think I got mixed up about it, but I was sure our neighborhood had been picked for something special—like my last big civic art piece, if that makes sense. And little things seemed to show it, like trucks slowing down by the house or neighbors looking over. It felt like they knew, too, so I couldn’t just leave it half-done. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be done in a very specific way to be considered complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it had to be a very specific way, doctor. Not like I had a written plan or rules in my head, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt was more… I kept feeling like it still wasn’t quite finished, so I’d add another thing or fix something that looked off. But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. But “everything around me” sounds like more than I’d want to claim. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t really see myself that way. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you generally found yourself to be a perfectionist in other areas of your life, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call myself a perfectionist, doctor. I mean… I’ve always liked things to be decent, done properly enough, but not perfect-perfect.\n\nWith the house, maybe I got more particular than usual. But in other areas? I don’t really think so. I might be missing something my family would notice, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve generally been that way if I’m not sure. Is that all right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had recurring thoughts or behaviors like this before, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. Unless I’m not understanding what you mean exactly. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting caught up in details, even in situations where it's not that important?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, anyway. I… I wouldn’t put it like something terrible exactly, maybe that sounds dramatic. But at the time it felt really important, like if I didn’t finish it the way I saw it in my head, the house—and even the block—would miss its chance to be something special.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess my family thought it was getting a little out of hand. I was working on the house, putting up wood and lights and display things, trying to make it really something special. They say I used up our savings on it, and I kept going after dark.\n\nI didn’t mean any harm, doctor. It felt important at the time, like if I stopped, the house—maybe even the whole block—would miss its chance. That probably sounds foolish, I know… but that’s why they wanted me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to keep working on the house, like you couldn't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, in a way. I didn’t think of it like I couldn’t stop, exactly. It felt more like I shouldn’t stop, if that makes sense.\n\nThey say I spent our savings on the wood and the lights and the display things, and I know that doesn’t look good. But at the time, I just kept feeling like if I quit for the night, the house—maybe even the whole block—would lose its chance at something important. So I kept pushing, even after dark.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any thoughts repeating in your mind that you couldn't get rid of while working on the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it thoughts repeating, doctor. Not like a phrase going round and round that I couldn’t get rid of.\n\nIt was more… I just kept thinking about what still needed doing on the house, and it felt hard to set it aside. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you were doing something over and over, like putting up the lights or the wood, because you felt you had to, not just because you wanted to?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, maybe that’s fair to say. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but I did keep putting up the wood and the lights and all the display things, even after dark.\n\nThey say I used our savings on it, which… I know doesn’t sound responsible. At the time, though, it didn’t feel like just something I wanted. It felt like if I stopped, the house—maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. Does that make sense, doctor?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any disturbing or intrusive thoughts that kept coming into your mind while you were working on the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, doctor… not disturbing thoughts, exactly. Nothing like something awful popping into my head that I couldn’t shake.\n\nI was mostly just focused on the house and what needed doing next. Maybe too focused, I can see that. But I wouldn’t want to say I had intrusive thoughts if that’s not the right word. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be perfect for this special occasion?\n\nPatient: Well… perfect might be too strong a word, doctor. I didn’t want it to sound like I was being fussy over every little thing.\n\nBut yes, it felt very important that it be right. I know people think I got mixed up about it, but I was sure our neighborhood had been picked for something special—like my last big civic art piece, if that makes sense. And little things seemed to show it, like trucks slowing down by the house or neighbors looking over. It felt like they knew, too, so I couldn’t just leave it half-done. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like the house had to be done in a very specific way to be considered complete?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it had to be a very specific way, doctor. Not like I had a written plan or rules in my head, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt was more… I kept feeling like it still wasn’t quite finished, so I’d add another thing or fix something that looked off. But I don’t know that I can point to one exact way it had to be. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. But “everything around me” sounds like more than I’d want to claim. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t really see myself that way. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you generally found yourself to be a perfectionist in other areas of your life, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call myself a perfectionist, doctor. I mean… I’ve always liked things to be decent, done properly enough, but not perfect-perfect.\n\nWith the house, maybe I got more particular than usual. But in other areas? I don’t really think so. I might be missing something my family would notice, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve generally been that way if I’m not sure. Is that all right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had recurring thoughts or behaviors like this before, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting caught up in details, even in situations where it's not that important?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, anyway. Does that answer it right?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you thought about stopping work on the house or not doing it exactly as you had envisioned?\n\nPatient: I suppose I did feel uncomfortable stopping, yes… though I didn’t think of it as anxiety at the time.\n\nI kept telling myself it just needed a little more, and if I stopped too soon, the house—and maybe the whole block—would miss its chance. They say I’m here because I spent our savings on wood and lights and display things, and because I kept working after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good, doctor. But at the time it felt important, like I was trying not to let everyone down. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say you're generally an inflexible person who likes to do things in a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m generally inflexible, no… at least I hope I don’t come across that way.\n\nI mean, I like things done right, same as anybody, but I wouldn’t say I’m the sort who has to have everything one particular way all the time. My family might have their own opinion, I suppose. But day to day, I don’t think of myself like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to be in control of everything that's going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I need to control everything, no… at least I wouldn’t say it that strongly.\n\nI like to know what’s happening, I guess, and if something feels important I may want to have a hand in it. But “everything around me” sounds like more than I’d want to claim. I’m not trying to be difficult—I just don’t really see myself that way. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you generally found yourself to be a perfectionist in other areas of your life, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call myself a perfectionist, doctor. I mean… I’ve always liked things to be decent, done properly enough, but not perfect-perfect.\n\nWith the house, maybe I got more particular than usual. But in other areas? I don’t really think so. I might be missing something my family would notice, but I wouldn’t want to say I’ve generally been that way if I’m not sure. Is that all right?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had recurring thoughts or behaviors like this before, not just with the house?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really think of, no. I mean, I’ve had habits like anybody does, I suppose, but nothing I’d call recurring thoughts or behaviors in the way you’re asking.\n\nThe house thing… that feels more like what stood out. Unless I’m not understanding what you mean exactly. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting caught up in details, even in situations where it's not that important?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not often, anyway. I might notice little things sometimes, especially if I’m already focused on something, but I don’t think I get stuck on details when it doesn’t matter.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve really noticed. If someone else saw it differently, maybe they’d say so, but from my side I wouldn’t want to make more of it than it is. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by others that you are overly meticulous or stubborn in your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think people have really told me that… not in those words, anyway. Maybe somebody might say I can be particular once in a while, but overly meticulous or stubborn in daily life? I don’t think that’s been a regular thing.\n\nI hope that makes sense—I’m trying to answer it straight, but I don’t want to overstate anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself insisting that others do things your way, even in situations where it's not crucial?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t believe so. I mean, I might have my preferences, like anyone, but I don’t think I push people to do things my way when it doesn’t really matter.\n\nIf I have done that, I’m not aware of it being a pattern. I’d hope people would tell me if I was coming across that way. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt compelled to perform specific rituals or routines in your daily life, outside of the situation with the house?\n\nPatient: Not that I can think of, no. I mean, I have little habits like anybody—doing things in a certain order sometimes—but I wouldn’t say I feel compelled, or like I have to do a ritual every day.\n\nOutside of the house situation, I don’t really notice anything like that. If you mean something more specific, you can ask it that way and I’ll try to answer it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you had to repeat certain actions or behaviors in other areas of your life, outside of the situation with the house?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really say I have. I mean, sometimes I might double-check something or do a thing again if I’m unsure, but not like I feel driven to repeat actions over and over in other parts of my life.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. If there’s a certain kind of thing you’re asking about, I can try to think on it a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something bad that would happen if you didn't complete the house decorations exactly as you envisioned?\n\nPatient: I… I wouldn’t put it like something terrible exactly, maybe that sounds dramatic. But at the time it felt really important, like if I didn’t finish it the way I saw it in my head, the house—and even the block—would miss its chance to be something special.\n\nThey say I’m here because I spent our savings on the wood, the lights, and all the display things, and because I kept working on it after dark. I know that doesn’t sound good. I just felt like stopping would ruin it somehow. Does that make sense, or am I saying it wrong?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like something bad would happen to you or others if you didn't complete the house decorations?\n\nPatient: Not like I thought someone would get hurt, no… at least I don’t think so.\n\nIt was more that if I stopped, I felt like the whole thing would be ruined somehow. Like the house, and maybe the block too, would lose this chance to be something special. I know that probably sounds odd. They say I used our savings on the wood and lights and the display things, and I kept going even after dark, and I can see why that worried people.\n\nBut in my mind then, it didn’t feel dangerous exactly—it felt like I couldn’t stop or I’d mess up something important. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like something was compelling you to continue decorating the house, even when you knew it was causing problems?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess it did feel that way, though I don’t want to make it sound stranger than it was.\n\nI knew people were upset, and they say I used our savings on the wood and the lights and all those display things, and that I kept pushing myself to work on it even after dark. I can see how that caused problems.\n\nBut at the time, stopping just felt… wrong. Like if I didn’t keep going, the whole house—and maybe the block—would lose its chance to be something really special. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
